personal contacts and all the modern conveniences and aids to keep the membership of the Church in close touch with the inspiration of heaven as it is revealed through the authorities of the Church. 

As long as we hearken to the inspiration and revelation of the Lord, as it comes through the one who is authorized to speak, we have little to fear, although there will arise among us, as there arose among the saints in primitive times, some who will not abide in the doctrines of the Church and will try to lead others into forbidden paths. We have the assurance that the Gospel of the Kingdom has been revealed and established on the earth for the last time, never again to be taken away although there will be some who will turn to modern fables and deny the power of God. Today all parts of the Church can be reached, even to the most remote part, in the course of two or three weeks, and the greater part within one or two days. Our advantage in this respect, should keep us from the errors which crept into the Primitive Church of Jesus Christ and proved its overthrow.

Paul's Warning to the Ephesian Saints. After 

Paul had completed his final missionary journey and had visited again the branches he and Barnabas first organized, and he was about to return to Jerusalem to attend the feast of Pentecost, he sent for the elders at Ephesus to come and meet him at Miletus as he did not have time to go to them. When they came Paul gave them

some very pointed and important instruction. other things he said: Among 

And now, behold, I know that ye all, among whom I have gone preaching the kingdom of God, shall see my face no more. 

Wherefore, I take you to record this day, that I am pure from the blood of all men. 

For I have not shunned to declare unto you all the counsel of God. 

Take heed therefore unto yourselves, and, to all the flock, over which the Holy Ghost hath made you overseers, to feed the church of God, which he hath purchased with his own blood.

For I know this, that after my departing shall grievous wolves enter in among you, not sparing the flock. 

Also of your own selves shall men arise, speaking perverse things, to draw away disciples after them.

Therefore watch, and remember, that by the space of three years I ceased not to warn every one night and day with tears. -Acts 20:25-32.

It is clearly seen from the epistles of these ancient apostles that they fully understood by the power of the 

Spirit that there was to come a falling away, or departure from the faith, and that they were not living in the day of the restoration of all things. Paul warned Timothy that the Spirit had expressly spoken to him declaring that "In the latter times some shall depart from the faith, giving heed to seducing spirits, and doctrines of devils."

Moreover these would have their consciences seared so that they would speak lies in hypocrisy; "forbidding to marry, and commanding to abstain from meats which

God hath created to be received with thanksgiving of them which believe and know the truth." 

Restoration Not For Their Day. Jesus informed his apostles on the day of his ascension that some things pertaining to his work were not intended for that dispensation. We read that on this occasion when they were come together they asked him, saying, "Lord, wilt thou at this time restore the kingdom to Israel?" His answer was, "It is not for you to know the times or the seasons, which the Father hath put in his own power."

In other words, they were not to be concerned with matters which were intended for a later dispensation. A short time after this Peter seemed to get the inspiration of this when speaking to the Jews who were guilty of crying out against the Son of God. He told them that there was no forgiveness or remission of sins for them at that time, hence they could not be baptized; but if they would repent perhaps there would come a time when some measure of forgiveness would be granted them. This time would be "when the times of refreshing shall come from the presence of the Lord," which would be at the time of "restitution of all things." This time of restitution was promised by the mouths of all the holy prophets since the world began, and Paul declared that this was to be in the dispensation of the fulness of times. These prophets clearly understood that the dispensation of the fulness of times was another dispensation remote from their dispensation.

The Seven Churches. 

The implication is very strong in the first three chapters of Revelation that at the time John received the vision there were only seven churches (i.e. branches of the Church) worthy of mention by the Lord. Moreover, it was necessary for the Lord to rebuke these churches which were the branches at 

Ephesus, Smyrna, Pergamos, Thyatira, Sardis, Philadelphia and Laodicea. While these branches were worthy of commendation for some things, yet most of them had to be rebuked for permitting iniquity to creep in among them, and be warned that unless repentance came their candlesticks would be removed. One of the seven, Laodicea, because it had shown indifference to the truth and was "neither cold nor hot," the Lord said he would "spue" out of his mouth. This charge to these seven churches gives us a fair insight into the conditions which prevailed among the members of the Church at the close of the first century. Is it not reasonable to believe that if there had been other branches of the Church worthy of recognition, the Lord would also have recognized them, either with commendation or rebuke? After this charge was given the Lord delivered this hopeful promise: "As many as I love, I rebuke and chasten; be zealous, therefore, and repent. Behold I stand at the door, and knock; if any man hear my voice, and open the door, I will come in to him, and will sup with him, and he with me. Το him that overcometh will I grant to sit with me in my throne, even as I also overcame, and am set down with

my Father in his throne. He that hath an ear to hear, let him hear what the Spirit saith unto the Churches."

Persecutions of the Early Christians. From the days of the crucifixion until the beginning of the fourth century, persecution followed the Saints. 

There were, of course, times of comparative peace, but Satan in his anger against the woman and her child, continued his labors of destruction until he thought he had accomplished his work. It was not long after the day of 

Pentecost that James, the son of Zebedee, was put to death. The activities of the Jews resulted in driving many of the disciples out of Palestine. This work was completed in the siege of Jerusalem by Titus. The greatest persecutions came, however, from the Romans. 

The strange thing about these persecutions is the fact that Rome had always been tolerant and unmindful of the religious beliefs of all nations. In their polytheistic worship they conceded the right to others to worship the gods of their own lands. Gibbons has said: "Rome gradually became the common temple of her subjects; and the freedom of the city was bestowed on all the gods of mankind. * * * The various modes of worship which prevailed in the Roman world were all considered by the people as being equally true; by the philosophers as all equally false; and by the magistrates as equally useful. And thus toleration produced not only mutual indulgence, but even religious concord." 

But with the true religion this was not so. The

While dragon was seeking to destroy the woman. He certainly stirred up the Roman rulers against the Church. toleration was granted to all others, there was no mercy for Christians. The first persecution, commencing in 

A.D. 64, took place under Nero, one of the lowest and most depraved of all reprobates. It was during this persecution that Peter and Paul suffered martydom. The second persecution came under the edict of Domitian in 94 A. D., and the third under Trajan. Others followed under Marcus Aurelius, Severus, Decius Trajan and down to the close of the reign of Diocletian at the beginning of the fourth century. The cruelties and horrors of these persecutions beggar description, and are passed over without any reference to detail. 

Effects of Persecution on the Church. These continued persecutions, some of which were given with exterminating edicts against all Christians no matter where they were found, brought thousands of the Saints to martyrdom. Many thousands renounced the faith to save their lives. Such methods, to bring to an end the work established by our Lord, proved to be unavailing so far as destroying the Church was concerned. That they weakened it, there can be no doubt, for many of the leading characters were removed by death, the officers were sought out and destroyed. But Satan discovered that there are other weapons far more effective than the sword, and fire, and burning at the stake.

The Conversion of Constantine, 

In the year

306 Constantine came to power in Rome. His father had protected the Christians in Gaul during the persecutions of Diocletian and the son, when he came to the throne, not only continued the policy of his father, but became a Christian. From that time on persecution ceased, and 

Christianity became a popular religion. In becoming popular, the Church lost it vitality and became clothed in the vestments of the Pagan world. The simplicity of the Gospel was lost in the maze and fog of mystic rites and ceremonies to the bewilderment of the people. With the power of the Roman government behind it, Christianity became the dominant religion in all of Europe. 

Rise of the Roman Pontiff. It is said that Constantine offered rewards to all who would become Chistians. Rome being the capital of the government, it was but natural that in the city of Rome there would be, under the circumstances, the greatest number of new members added. In course of time the local ecclesiastical government in Rome claimed pre-eminence and the right to direct in the affairs of all other branches of the Church. The story of the fight for this distinction and how, eventually, other branches of the Church submitted to the domination of Rome, is too long a story to be given here. It is sufficient to say that by weight of influence and numbers, Rome succeeded in impressing this upon all other branches and subjected them to her will. In the days of the apostles the several branches, called churches, were presided over by local officers,

who in turn were presided over by other officers, similar to the order in the Church today. These were all subject to the regulation of the Twelve Apostles. The authority in Rome assumed greater and greater power until the claim was made that the bishop of Rome was, and had been since the death of Peter, the successor to the chief apostle in the government of the Church. From that day this order was submitted to, and, to this day, that doctrine has been maintained in the Catholic Church. 

Changes in the Church. The Church established by our Lord and his apostles was a simple organization free from all pomp, mystery and ostentation. Its members were humble and met on a common plane. The priest was no better than the member, although he was respected in his calling. All were one in their simplicity and brotherly relationships. The Church was free from pride and class distinction. All of this was rudely changed after the death of the apostles. Offices never dreamed of in the true Church were created. Ceremonies foreign to the Gospel were added. Ordinances instituted for the salvation of men were discarded or changed. By the multiplicity of changes in ordinances, principles, and church government, the Church of Jesus Christ ceased to exist on the earth. Satan had accomplished far more effectively, in times of peace, what he failed to do by persecution and making martyrs in the Church. Power, wealth, pride and the wearing of costly raiment became the order of the times. This splendor and magnificence

displayed by the officials in the church as it then existed surpassed anything the world had seen before. The people were taught to bow in reverence and awe of their priests, contrary to the order of heaven and which an angel would not permit John to do before him, because he was also one of the brethren who had the testimony of Jesus.

The Savior brought an accusation against the scribes and Pharisees and the rulers of the Jews of his day, because they sought after all of these worldly things, but the fifth century saw the Church of Rome surpassing all things in its wealth and splendor. The Gospel ordinances had been changed; the new and everlasting covenant had been broken; the Priesthood had been taken back to God; the Church had fled into the wilderness, and the true government of God had been driven from the earth, to remain until, in the dispensation of the fulness of times, it was to be restored again.

CHAPTER 15  THROUGH TRANSGRESSION 

We have 

Man Was Intelligent in the Beginning. learned from the writings of Abraham and Moses, which the Lord has restored to "as many as believe," that man in the morning of time was an intelligent being. Adam was chosen for his mission in this mortal life before the foundations of the earth were laid. His appointment came because he was one of the greatest of the intelligences in the spirit world. He merited, by his faithfulness and intelligence, according to our scriptures, the right to be chosen to come to this earth as "the first man" and "father of all." He was the Ancient of Days because he was the first man, and by virtue of his calling became a prince over his posterity forever. He was "Michael, the prince, the archangel," who helped to form this earth for himself and his posterity. 1 

Adam the Ancient of Days. Speaking of Adam the Prophet Joseph Smith has said: 

Commencing with Adam, who was the first man, who is spoken of in Daniel as being the Ancient of Days, or in other words, the first and oldest of all, the great grand progenitor of whom it is said in another place he is Michael, because he was the first and father of all, not only by progeny, but the first to hold the spiritual blessings, to whom was made known the plan of ordinances for the salvation of his posterity unto the end,

and to whom Christ was first revealed, and through whom Christ has been revealed from heaven, and will continue to be revealed henceforth. Adam holds the keys of the dispensaton of the fulness of times, i.e., the dispensation of all times have been and will be revealed through him from the beginning to Christ, and from Christ to the end of all the dispensations that are to be revealed. *** 

And again: God purposed in himself that there should not be an eternal fulness until every dispensation should be fulfilled and gathered together in one, and that all things whatsoever, that should be gathered together in one in those dispensations unto the same fulness and eternal glory, should be in Christ

Jesus; therefore he set the ordinances to be the same forever and ever, and set Adam to watch over them, to reveal them from heaven to man, or to send angels to reveal them. * * 

These angels are under the direction of Michael or Adam, who acts under the direction of the Lord.-History of the Church 4:207.

Adam Holds Keys of All Generations. 

On one other occasion the Prophet Joseph Smith, discoursing on 

Priesthood, said:

The Priesthood was first given to Adam; he obtained the 

First Presidency, and held the keys of it from generation to generation. He obtained it in the creation, before the world was formed, as in Genesis 1:26-28. He had dominion given him over every living creature. He is Michael, the Archangel, spoken of in the scripture. Then to Noah, who is Gabriel; he stands next in authority to Adam in the Priesthood, he was called of God to this office, and was the father of all living in his day, and to him was given the dominion. These men held keys first on earth, and then in heaven. * * *

Daniel in his seventh chapter speaks of the Ancient of 

Days; he means the oldest man, our father Adam, Michael, he

will call his children together and hold a council with them to prepare them for the coming of the Son of Man. He, Adam, is the father of the human family, and presides over the spirits of all men, and all that have had the keys must stand before him in this grand council.-History of the Church 3:386-388. 

These sayings of the Prophet's concerning Adam are confirmed in the Doctrine and Covenants. 

In a revelation given in March, 1832, we are informed that 

Jesus Christ established Adam-ondi-Ahman, and "appointed Michael, your prince, and established his feet, and set him upon high, and gave him the keys of salvation, under the direction of Jesus Christ, the Holy

One who is without beginning of days or end of life." (D. and C. 78:15-16.) We are also informed (D. and 

C. 107) that "three years before the death of Adam he called Seth, Enos, Cainan, Mahalaleel, Jared, Enoch, and Methuselah, who were all High Priests, with the residue of fris posterity who were righteous, into the valley of Adam-ondi-Ahman, and there bestowed upon them his last blessing. And the Lord appeared unto them, and they rose up and blessed Adam, and called him Michael, the prince, the archangel. And the Lord administered comfort unto Adam, and said unto him:

I have set thee to be at the head; a multitude of nations shall come of thee, and thou art a prince over them forever. And Adam stood up in the midst of the congregation; and, notwithstanding he was bowed down with age, being full of the Holy Ghost, predicted what-

soever should befall his posterity unto the latest generation." 

Adam Had a Perfect Language. We are also informed in the writings of Moses that Adam was taught a perfect language under the tutelage of God, while 

Adam was in the Garden of Eden. Moreover, in that day when men began to multiply Adam was still among them And a book of remembrance was kept, in the which was recorded, in the language of Adam, for it was given unto as many as called upon God to write by the spirit of inspiration. 

And by them their children were taught to read and write, having a language which was pure and undefiled. 

Some centuries later when Enoch was teaching the people he said:

And death hath come upon our fathers; nevertheless we know them and cannot deny, and even the first of all we know, even Adam. For a book of remembrance we have written among us, according to the pattern given by the finger of God; and it is given in our own language.

From these writings we discover that in the very beginning men were intelligent, possessed of knowledge and wisdom and were clothed upon by the power of the Holy Ghost. Moreover, the Gospel in its fulness was had among them. They had a powerful language, for it was the language of God, perfect in both the spoken and the written word. We also learn that those antediluvian patriarchs from the days of Adam were

acquainted with the creation of this earth and with the heavenly bodies, their revolutions and the purpose of their creation. by revelation.

All of these things were made known The reason for presenting in some detail these facts at the beginning of this lesson, is to impress upon the reader that God did not leave man in an ignorant, helpless and uncivilized state, in the beginning of this world; and to show that the Almighty is ever ready to teach the people and to give them intelligent understanding of many things considered in the world to be in the realm of science and art, with which it is said by many today the Creator does not bother himself, but leaves man to work out for himself. If men will only hearken to the Spirit of Truth and accepted the revelations of the Lord as they have been made known to the prophets, he will inspire them in all things for their temporal as well as for their spiritual salvation. 

Abraham's Knowledge. From Abraham we receive further light regarding the knowledge and wisdom of 

Adam and his posterity before the flood. Abraham says:

But the records of the fathers, even the patriarchs, concerning the right of Priesthood, the Lord my God preserved in mine own hands; therefore a knowledge of the beginning of the creation, and also of the planets, and of the stars as they were made known unto the fathers, have I kept, even unto this day, and I shall endeavor to write some of these things upon this record, for the benefit of my posterity that shall come after me.

In these writings of Abraham we discover that he had a most wonderful knowledge of the heavens and of the earth and their relationship to each other. Part of this he obtained from the writings of the fathers and part from his own research and from revelation. No astronomer since his day has been in possession of such knowledge of the heavens as was revealed to Abraham by the Lord through Urim and Thummim. By this means Abraham learned of the relationship of the stars to each other and that there were great governing ones. which were set "near the throne of God." The earth upon which we dwell is subject to the governing influence of Kolob, as are all those of our solar system. Kolob is the greatest of all the Kokaubeam and is nearest the throne of God. Abraham saw these great stars and learned of their revolutions, their days and years, according to their reckoning. Abraham taught this knowledge of astonomy to the Egyptians. We know from these ancient records that in the beginning and down through the ages among the people of God there was a correct understanding in relation to the universe. 

The Nephite Knowledge of the Universe. From the Book of Mormon we learn that this knowledge was had among the Nephites. Mormon, commenting upon the power of God and the weakness and ingratitude of man, has said:

Yea, and if he say unto the earth--Move-it is moved.

Yea, and if he say unto the earth-Thou shalt go back, that it lengthen out the day for many hours-it is done; 

And thus, according to his word the earth goeth back, and it appearth unto man that the sun standeth still; yea, and behold, this is so; for surely it is the earth that moveth and not the sun.

The knowledge of astronomy was well developed among the ancients upon the American continent. There were scholars among them acquainted with the arts and sciences, which truth is shown in their wonderful buildings, monuments and sculptures. Among the evidences of their advanced knowledge of the heavenly bodies and of mathematics, we have before us their wonderful system of canals cut for hundreds of miles through the Andes and in other parts of the land, and the Mayan Calendar Stone, superior to anything known on the eastern hemisphere. So accurately was this calendar worked out that the people could fix any date within a period of 374,000 years.

In their excellent work on "The 

History of the Maya," Thomas Gann and J. Eric

Thompson have this to say: "The Maya calendar can rightly be described as one of the greatest, indeed one might say the greatest, achievement in the line of pure reason science ever achieved by a people of the same cultural horizon as the Mayas." The historian Prescott has spoken of this culture and civilization as being equal to that of the Spaniards, and in many respects superior to that of their conquerors who came from the Old World. 

False Ideas of Today. We know, then, that the

prevalent notion in the world today that the ancients from the dawn of history to the days of Galileo believed that the earth is flat and the center of the universe, around which the sun and stars revolve, is a most stupendous error. The ignorance made manifest in the days of Galileo and Columbus, and for centuries preceding their time, was the result of apostasy from and rebellion against the truth which in the beginning was plainly revealed from the heavens. 

We know that Jesus Christ-Jehovah-framed this earth under the command of his Father. Moreover, worlds without number he created. This was taught to Adam, Moses, Abraham, and John the Beloved, the latter having written:

In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God. 

The same was in the beginning with God. 

All things were made by him; and without him was not anything made that was made.

In him was life; and the life was the light of men. 

When our Lord was in his ministry he organized his Church and taught his Gospel. Surely, he who made the earth, and all things, was not ignorant of the nature of the universe. The truth regarding the earth he taught to his disciples. They understood it as they understood the fundamental principles of the Gospel. But after they had passed away, and the author of sin and wickedness had taken possession of affairs and had driven the

Church into the wilderness, as predicted by the prophets, then there set in a reaction which culminated in gross ignorance of heavenly things. This falling away also had noticeable effect upon the arts and sciences. This loss of knowledge concerning God, the fundamental principles of the Gospel, the earth and the heavens, came about in a most natural way. Darkness always follows in the wake of sin. Ignorance is the offspring of rebellion against light and truth. Idolatry is Satan's weapon aimed at the destruction of belief in God and the atoning sacrifice of his Only Begotten Son. The history of the Lamanites and Nephites upon this American continent has been paralleled many times in other parts of the earth when men rejected revealed truth. 

The descendants of the Mayas and Aztecs, in the day of the Spanish conquest, could not build the magnificent monuments and calculate as did their enlightened fathers. 

The Lord Always Willing To Impart Knowledge. It is true that many of the false ideas and erroneous scientific theories and beliefs of the dark ages were held by various peoples before the time of Christ; but these false teachings and practices were always developed from the seed of transgression and apostasy. Our Father in heaven has always been willing to aid his children in correct understanding, not only of the plan of salvation, but of all other essential things, to aid man in this mortal life. He has commanded us to seek diligently "words

of wisdom," out of the best books, and to seek learning "even by study and also by faith."

Elder Parley P. Pratt in his inspired little work, "The Key to Theology," has very clearly pointed out that through the science of theology-which is the greatest of all sciences-the prophets of God have been taught not only to understand the plan of salvation, but many things pertaining to their temporal salvation as well. Elder Pratt declares that the science of theology is:

The science by which worlds are organized, sustained and directed and the elements controlled.

It is the science of knowledge, and the key and power thereof, by which the heavens are opened, and lawful access is obtained to the treasures of wisdom and intelligence—inexhaustible, infinite, embracing the past, the present, and the future. 

It is the science of life, endless and eternal. 

It is the science of faith, reformation and remission of sins, whereby a fallen race of mortals may be justified, cleansed, and restored to the communion and fellowship of the Holy Spirit. 

It is the science of spiritual gifts, by which the blind see, the deaf hear, the lame walk, the sick are healed, and demons are expelled from the human system.

It is the science of all other sciences and useful arts, being in fact, the very fountain from which they emanate. It includes philosophy, astronomy, history, mathematics, geography, languages, the science of letters, and blends the knowledge of all matters of fact, in every branch of art, or research. It includes, also, all the scientific discoveries and inventions, agriculture, the mechanical arts, architecture, shipbuilding, the properties and applications of the mariner's compass, navigation and music. All

that is useful, great and good, all that is calculated to sustain, comfort, instruct, edify, purify, refine or exalt intelligence, originated by this science, and this science alone, all other sciences being but branches growing out of this, the root. 

Rebellion of Men Against God. Elder Pratt continues and shows that the Almighty taught man the art of agriculture; he made coats and introduced the tailor's art. He commanded and gave pattern for Noah's Ark, thus introducing shipbuilding. He revealed the pattern of the Tabernacle, and afterwards the plan of the Temple of Solomon. The Lord wrote with his own finger on the 

Tables of Stone on Mount Sinai. The Lord introduced the surveyor's art in the building of cities, temples and other buildings, and by this science all things are made known. 

And so, we see from the revelations of the Lord, that he is ever willing to guide the people and teach them the useful arts, sciences, and his eternal word, by which the fulness of knowledge comes, if the people will but have it so. Unfortunately the people almost everywhere on the earth from the beginning have not willed it so. 

They have rebelled from the beginning because Satan came among them saying: "I am also a son of God; and he commanded them saying: Believe it not; and they believed it not; and they loved Satan more than God. 

And men began from that time forth to be carnal, sensual and devilish." This course of unbelief and willingness to follow the prince of darkness who was,

we are told, "a liar from the beginning," and would that all men might be miserable like unto himself, led mankind to do all manner of wickedness which resulted in ignorance and spiritual darkness concerning the things connected with the kingdom of God. We are taught that it is only through the power of the Priesthood that the Gospel is administered and the keys of the knowledge of God and of his power are made manifest. Without the ordinances and the Priesthood the power of godliness is not made manifest to men in the flesh. (D. and C. 

84: 19-21.) Therefore, when men turn from the power of the Priesthood-through which the heavens are opened and the Creator is revealed-and refuse to accept the ordinances of the Gospel, darkness takes hold of their minds and drives out the light of truth. This is the reason why the Savior said: "Take heed therefore how ye hear: for whosoever hath, to him shall be given; and whosoever hath not, from him shall be taken even that which he seemeth to have."

The Gospel Ordinances Changed. It was not long after the death of the Apostles that the evil influences which had been predicted by Peter and Paul, yes, and by our Redeemer also, set in and soon accomplished their work. When Christianity was made the state religion of Rome, then Paganism was mixed with the Gospel and material changes were made in the principles and ordinances. Mankind lost the true knowledge of 

God and the power by which that knowledge is manifest.

It was not long afterwards when the creed of Athanasius replaced the simple and understandable doctrine of God taught by his Only Begotten Son. Not only was this knowledge lost and the principles of the Gospel corrupted with the philosophies of the Pagan world, but there came also a decline in all the spiritual gifts. The ordinance of baptism was wickedly changed; sprinkling and pouring were substituted for the burial in the water in similitude of the death and resurrection of Jesus Christ. The declaration was made that the bread and wine became actually by transubstantiation the actual body and blood of Christ. 

Likewise other grave and fatal changes were made in the ordinances and doctrines of the Church.

There came also a decline in art, science, literature, and all things uplifting and beneficial to the mortal life. 

Only a select few were granted the privilege of an education, such as those times afforded. Few outside the clergy were educated enough to read and write. For this reason the priests became the lawyers, diplomats, instructors, dictators to kings, and controllers of affairs of state. Morever, the temporal, social and spiritual affairs of the people were controlled by the priests. All learned men talked and wrote in Latin, which the common people did not understand; nor was it intended that they should understand. It was the teachings of the times that the people must not comprehend and properly interpret the sacred word. The scriptures were

said to be the exclusive property of the priests, or clergy, and their use among the laity was frowned upon. No part of the scriptures was to be read in public or in private by those not of the clergy. Neither was any part or fragment of the scriptures to be in the possession of the common people on pain of death. Discussion of the word of the Lord was forbidden in the home and prayers were prescribed by the clergy for the people. 

Many other abuses were introduced and the rights and freedom of the people taken away. Is there any wonder that under such conditions there should come an age of darkness and ignorance upon the land?

Erroneous Belief Regarding Learning. For cen turies the opinion has commonly prevailed that in the days of Christ and in olden times, the art of writing and reading was confined to the few who were designated as scribes, lawyers, and the rulers of the people. Recent archeological research has corrected this view. It has been discovered from the abundance of records which have now been found that the common people in these ages could read and write and that other knowledge was widely disseminated even back in early times. Dr. Camden M. Coburn, in his valuable work, "The New 

Archeological Discoveries," page 99, has said:

In the lifetime of our Lord there was a widespread habit of writing among all classes of the population, and no doubt the leading facts were written down and circulated "almost as soon as they took place,"-though doubtless at first in fragmentary form so that probably the first account of the death of

Jesus "must be presumed to be written in the year he died." (Milligan). As soon as the converts became so many that the original apostles could not easily carry authoritative facts personally to all Christian communities, a need of records would be felt which, because of this general habit of note taking and writing, could be readily supplied.

He also adds, as have other archaeological investigators, that "the Gospels were all written before the destruction of Jerusalem, 70 A. D." Other writers have declared that writing was common even in the days of Moses and before, not only among the Israelites, but among other nations.

The Dark Ages. 

Attention is called to this to show the contrast which came upon the so-called Christian world during the time known as the dark ages. It is generally understood that the dark ages commenced with the fall of Rome and continued during the greater part of the next thousand years. The first five or six hundred years of this period are considered the darkest.

Professor Myers, in his History of the Middle Ages, has said:

Long before the fall of Rome there was a very observable decline of the Latin intellect. Science, literature, philosophythe entire intellectual as well as political side of Roman civilization, showed evidence of weakness and decay. Then came the inrush of the barbarians, and the countries that had been brought under the influence of Latin culture and refinement sank back into almost primitive ignorance and rudeness. The lowest point of depression was touched probably in the seventh century:

Hallam calls that the nadir of the human mind in Europe.

Some modern historians have tried to account for this decline in the fact that the barbarian hosts swept over Europe and subdued Rome. They have said that these barbarians were not sufficiently developed in intellect in those times to grasp the high culture and civilization which had existed during the days of the glory of Greece and Rome. Nothing could be farther from the truth. The intellect of the barbarian was just as keen and alert and capable as were the intellects of the civilizations which had gone on before. The reason for this age of extreme darkness was due to the fact of apostasy and departure from the light of truth-a turning from the simple and enlightening principles of the Gospel. Neither should we lose sight of the fact that in that period the learning which had been freely granted to peoples in other times, including the privilege to worship, read and write, had been denied the people. 

Their very prayers were prepared for them. Their form of worship was made incomprehensible, and they were so overawed by the pomp and mysticism of the clergy as to be held in abject ignorance and superstition. This condition was only natural, considering the times. 

Another writer, Dean R. W. Church, in the History of the Middle Ages, has brought in this indictment of the times:

The times, from the fifth to the eighth century, offer an example of a real catastrophe of strange and rare violence in the progress of mankind. On such a scale and with such results

it has only happened once. It stands alone, as far as we know, among the revolutions and changes of the world. *** Changes as great have been since, but they have been gradual. Convulsions almost as terrific have also happened; but they have been partial. But then for more than three centuries, it seems as if the world and human society had been hopelessly wrecked, without prospect or hope of escape. And what gave to this misery additional bitterness, was that there was always a considerable number of persons, sufficiently imbued with the ideas and imagination of a happier time, to be alive to the contrast, and to feel more acutely the wretchedness and despair of the present. 

The language of the Psalms alone adequately represents such feelings: "The earth is moved, the hills are carried in the midst of the sea. All the foundations of the earth are out of course." 

P. 43.

They did not care in such days to cultivate the powers and refinements of language, and they soon lost what they had inherited of those powers and refinements; they lost, too, with this, the generalizing and comparing faculties, the value for exactness, for proportion, for adequacy of statement. The Teutonic conquest was followed by centuries in which we see an increasing literary depression, and a universal incapacity for efforts of strong and fruitful thought. p. 61.

The condition of mental and spiritual stupor and stupidity was not due to a lack of mental capacity, but to the power which Satan exercised over the people. As the Lord said to the Nephites after the great hour of destruction which came to them, so we may in truth say of the people of these times. Satan laughed, and his angels rejoiced because of the power which they had obtained over the fair sons and daughters of the people, and this because of transgression of the sacred laws of divine will which had been proclaimed by the Son of

God to lift the human family to a higher plane through the blessings of the Gospel. day. 

Fortunately there was to come the dawn of a better

CHAPTER 16 THE  Indifference Towards Learning. Henry W. Elson in "Modern Times and the Living Past," has said: "The great majority of the people during the Middle Ages were wholly without an education. They lived their simple life on their farms as their fathers and grandfathers had done; they had their games and their festivals, they served their feudal landlords and paid heavy taxes, but they cared nothing for books and learning and knew little of the great world around them."

During these dark times there was very little to give encouragement to or create an incentive for the common people to seek learning. Since all books were written by hand, a very tedious practice, and were therefore limited and exceedingly expensive, all such were beyond the meager means of the common people. 

Moreover, such publications were in the Latin language. 

Translations of the Bible were chained to the pulpits in the churches and accessible only to the priests who guarded them with a jealous eye. In those days if a man was accused of a crime and he could read and write, and of course this would be in Latin, he was entitled to a trial before the ecclesiastical courts, where to convict required the testimony of numerous witnesses. 

If a layman was accused two witnesses only were sufficient

to convict. Learning on the part of the common people was discouraged and looked upon as the special prerogative of the clergy.

Springtime of the Renaissance. 

Conditions of this kind could not endure forever. The Lord never intended that man should be kept in ignorance. The time had to come when the minds of men were to be freed from the chains that enslaved them. There came then, the time for a revival of learning which is generally spoken of as The Renaissance, or new birth. This period marks the division between the Middle Ages and what we are pleased to call the beginning of modern times. John 

Addington Symonds in "The Beginning and Progress of The Renaissance," has said: "The Word 'Renaissance' has of late years received a more expanded significance than that which is implied in our English equivalentthe 'Revival of Learning.' We use it to denote the whole transition from the Middle Ages to the modern world; and though it is possible to assign certain limits to the period during which the transition took place, we cannot fix on any dates so positively as to say between this year and that the movement was accomplished. To do so would be like trying to name the day on which spring in any particular season began and ended. Yet we speak of spring as different from winter and summer. 

However, it is generally stated that near the end of the fifteenth and beginning of the sixteenth century formed the real springtime of this era. Following the

twelfth century the first dawn of light began to appear. 

The world was like a great giant who gradually began to stir from a long drunken stupor, sit up, rub his eyes and look about him, hold his aching head and try to work the stiffness out of his joints, and realize the awful plight he had been in; then gradually as the blood began to course normally he began once again to find himself and put himself in order with his surroundings. Let us not forget that the hand of the Lord was over the people and the set time for the dawn of a better day had come by divine decree." "Nothing Takes Place by Accident." Henry

George Bohn has very truly said when speaking of these times: "Nothing in the great world which concerns the well being of man takes place by accident, but is brought forward by divine will, precisely at the moment most suitable to our condition. So it was with astronomy, the 

Mariner's compass, the steam engine, gas, the electric telegraph, and many other of those blessings which have progressed with civilization. The elements were there and known, but the time had not arrived for their fructification."

We know that the Lord opened the spiritual eyes and understanding of Enoch and Moses so that they each beheld the inhabitants of the earth to the end of time. It is written: 

And it came to pass, as the voice was speaking, Moses cast his eyes and beheld the earth, yea, even all of it; and there was

not a particle of it which he did not behold, discerning it by the Spirit of God. And he beheld also the inhabitants thereof, and there was not a soul which he beheld not; and he discerned them by the Spirit of God; and their numbers were great, even numberless as the sands upon the sea shore.

Enoch saw the inhabitants also to the end of time. 

Both of these prophets saw the times of peace, rebellion and wickedness of the peoples which brought to pass, or will bring to pass, their destruction. Similar visions were granted to the brother of Jared, to Nephi and to 

John the Beloved, each of whom wrote concerning the history of the inhabitants of the earth to the end of time. 

It can hardly be disputed that these prophets so privileged saw the manners and customs of the peoples of future generations, their wars and contentions, their methods of warfare and means of transportation, and many other events showing modern discoveries and inventions. Isaiah must have seen in vision our railroad trains and other means of rapid transportation. It seems that he saw the western hemisphere as it would be in the last days, the land "shadowing with wings," or in the shape of wings, sending ambassadors in swift vessels to a "nation scattered and peeled," to proclaim to them the gathering to their home land once again. Nahum apparently saw the modern automobiles as they "jostled one against another in the broad ways," and as they ran like lightning and seemed like torches. The Lord certainly has known the end from the beginning. All

discoveries and inventions have come because he willed it and as said by Dr. Bohn, these things did not take place by accident, but in the time and place of the appointment. In

The Spirit of the Lord Among the People. these latter days it is easy for members of the Church to understand how the Spirit of the Lord was working among the people in those former times. The awakening in the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries, which took on momentum in the following three centuries, was the dawning of the day in which the fulness of freedom and religious liberty was to be ushered in. All the discoveries and inventions which followed so rapidly one upon another, were to make preparation for the restoration of the Gospel once again in its fulness. Of this, however, more will be said in another place. At this time we will consider some of the outstanding events belonging to the period of the Renaissance.

Long before the awakening in Europe there had been progress in literature, art and science, among the Saracens, who "during the dark ages, were almost the sole repositories of the scientific knowledge of the world. 

A part of this they gathered for themselves, for the Arabian scholars were original investigators, but a large share of it they borrowed from the Greeks. While the 

Western nations were too ignorant to know the value of the treasures of antiquity, the Saracens preserved them by translating into Arabic the scientific works of

Aristotle, the treatises on medicine by Galen, and the astronomical writings of the Alexandrian Greeks; and then, when Europe was prepared to appreciate these accumulations of the past, gave them back to her." (Myers.)

Effect of the Crusades on Learning. The Cru sades were great military expeditions by the Christians of 

Europe intended to wrest from the Mohammedan the

Holy Land. They were inspired by religious fervor and fanaticism and the love of adventure on the part of the soldier who was trained to war. The first Crusade was undertaken in 1096 and there were seven others from time to time until 1272. These Crusades, while they failed in the purpose for which they were conducted, did result in an enlarged view and understanding on the part of the people of Europe. East and West were brought nearer to each other. The knights and soldiers who engaged in these wars brought to the people of Europe many thoughts and ideas about the world that were new to them. These experiences also had a tendency to break down the feudal aristocracy and enlarge the powers of the kings. The increased knowledge of geography, a better understanding of the Mohammedan and the contact with the learning of the East, through the Crusades, stimulated the desire for further knowledge. 

The Adventure of Marco Polo. Another influence which stimulated the sluggish European mind was the remarkable tale told by Marco Polo in the last quarter

of the thirteenth century. Marco Polo, a Venetian traveler, returned to Italy after some twenty years spent in the far East. He wrote a book containing many remarkable experiences, describing the people, their customs, industries, etc. Then in the middle of the fourteenth century another adventurer appeared; he was 

Sir John Mandevil, an Englishman, who, according to his account, spent some thirty years and more among the peoples of the East. His tales also stimulated the minds of the people to seek further knowledge of the strange peoples living in lands of far off mystery. 

Trade With the East. As the knowledge began to dawn upon the Europeans regarding the peoples of other lands, and as there were brought into their communities many new things in the form of merchandise, the desire was increased to have more intimate intercourse with these people of the East. In very early times, drugs, silks, spices, etc., were obtained by the Greeks and other peoples from India. Such articles had to be brought by caravans. The long tedious journeys, which wer fraught with much danger, made such journeys very difficult. During the Middle Ages the volume of this trade was very small and consisted of articles of luxury for the wealthy classes. The Crusades and the adventures of such men as Polo and Mandevil, stimulated the desire for trade. When the Ottoman Turks in the fifteenth century captured Constaninople, trade over land became too precarious. Some other means of

approach to the East had to be devised. Most of the trade had been carried on by the cities of Venice and Genoa, which, during the Crusades, reaped a rich harvest. 

When the trade routes were threatened by the hostility of the Turks, the Genoese sought a way by sailing through the Straits of Gibraltar. Prince Henry of Portugal was one of the earliest navigators in the Atlantic 

Ocean. Following his expeditions down the African coast, Bartolomew Dias in 1486 rounded the Cape of Good Hope, thus proving that the wonderful nightmares which had afflicted the people concerning the tropical seas, were imaginary. In 1498, after Columbus had been to the western world, Vasco de Gama made a successful voyage to India from whence most of the merchandise from the East had come.

The Mariner's Compass. These extended voyages resulted in the building of better ships, and when the Mariner's compass came into general use, long voyages could be safely taken. Before this time the mariners endeavored to keep their ships within sight of land, or, if venturing beyond, to be sure they knew their position by the stars. Dreadful stories were current in relation to the dangers of the sea beyond the European shores. Dragons and dreadful monsters, enemies of man, infested the Sea of Darkness, as the Atlantic was called.

Should a vessel venture too far to the west, it could never return for it would become a prey to these awful monsters. Then, again, when men began to consider

that perhaps the earth was a globe, they declared that no ship could stay on the under side and should they get down they never could come up to the top again. 

When the Mariner's compass was first discovered is not certainly known. It has been said that the Chinese knew something about the polaric influence of the loadstone before the Christian Era, but this is not positively proved. The discovery that a loadstone will tend to direct itself towards the north and the south was known long before the compass was brought into use for navigation. This knowledge seems to have been derived by the Europeans from the Arabs. Whatever the origin may be, the fact remains that some time in the twelfth century the use of the magnetic needle in navigation was known, but not generally used until a later time. This principle was well understood in the days of De Gama and Columbus. 

Like the dis 

The Discovery of Gunpowder. 

covery of the compass and many other wonderful things, the origin of gunpowder remains a mystery. The Chinese have been given credit for this discovery. Whether or not this is so is immaterial, but the proof is lacking. The use of gunpowder is thought to be a development rather than a sudden discovery. As early as the seventh century an incendiary composition known as "Greek fire," was used in the navy of the Eastern Empire. This consisted of salt, sulphur, pitch, rosin and oil. The salt and the petroleum were obtained from the vicinity of Constanti-

nople. This substance was thrown or placed in tubes and dropped on the vessels of the enemy at the time of ramming them. These tubes used were similar to the breechloading guns of later days. The first mention of the composition of dry powder is made by Roger Bacon in the thirteenth century. Its application in times of war is ascribed to a German, Schwartz, by name, in 1330, but it is also claimed that even before this time it was used by the Turks. At the battle of Crecy in 

1346, it is said the English had three small cannon. The use of gunpowder, when it became general, brought to an end the age of Chivalry and its medieval castles with moats and bridges. No longer was it possible to conduct the splendid tournament with its knights tilting and rushing at each other on gaily caparisoned horses amidst the excited shouts of the assembled throng. The use of plated or chained armor and the shield came to an end. Eventually it also brought the introduction of more powerful weapons of war. Then ships had to be reconstructed on a large and stronger scale. The horrors of war became more terrible, the end of which, unfortunately, the world has not seen. 

What a shame it is that useful things have to be perverted to unnatural and evil ends and to add to the sorrow and affliction of the people!

CHAPTER 17  Benefits From Accumulated Knowledge. It is not the purpose of this discussion to chronicle all the important steps taken in the development and revival of knowledge known as the Renaissance, but merely to point out some of the high lights which played such an important part in this revival. We must pass by hurriedly many important events with scarcely a mention, such as the part played by Lorenzo De Medici, under whose influence Florence became the center of learning and has been called by some writers the "Modern 

Athens;" the wonderful skill of Michael Angelo and

Raphael in the field of art; the early reformers and the skillful teachers in different parts of Europe, such as Erasmus, Colet, More, and many others, whose influence was felt not only in their own but succeeding generations. From the commencement of the revival many agencies were at work, each doing its particular part in the great work of reconstruction and restoration which was to bring back to the world much of the culture and knowledge which had been lost through darkness which came naturally because of the rejection of the inspiration of the Holy Spirit and the revealed word of the Lord.

The world has benefited wonderfully by the accumulation of knowledge and experience coming down from the distant past. If men had continued to walk humbly in paths of righteousness this knowledge and experience would have saved the world from the spiritual and educational darkness which fell upon it with such force following the death of the apostles and early elders of the Primitive Church of Jesus Christ. 

Much that is vital to the welfare of mankind was lost because of rebellion, stubbornness and greed. The Lord has declared that he gives to "every man that cometh into the world," the "Spirit of Jesus Christ," which is also known as the "Light of Truth." This Spirit is given to guide "every man through the world that hearkeneth to the voice of the Spirit. And every one that hearkeneth to the voice of the Spirit cometh unto God, even the Father.” Because of the rebellion of men this 

Spirit withdraws from them and therefore they do not come unto God; neither do they accept his commandments as they have been given in olden times, even from the beginning. In this way man fails to take advantage of the teachings and experiences of the past and is forced to suffer many trials and partake of many deprivations because of his stubbornness and hardness of heart. 

We have

Records From the Earliest Times. 

learned that records have been kept from the very earliest times. In fact the keeping of records has been definitely

and sacredly commanded by the Lord and required of his people in all ages since man was first placed upon the earth. The keeping of such records was not merely for the benefit of the generation in which the writings were made, but for the benefit of all future generations. It is very refreshing to the Latter-day Saints to know that in the beginning of the history of this world the Lord taught his children to read and write. Moreover, that the records which were written were in a language pure and undefiled. In this language all matters of doctrine, history and genealogy, having a bearing on the welfare of the people through the ages, were inscribed. Many of these important matters have been withheld from the world in its days of wickedness. We, today, are still waiting for the restoration of the words of the Lord to Enoch and to the Brother of Jared and others who received important information and doctrine which they were commanded to write. This history and doctrine is withheld from us because of the wickedness of the generation in which we live, for the Lord has said it "shall not go forth unto the Gentiles until the day that they shall repent of their iniquity, and become clean before the Lord." In the days of Adam a "book of remembrance" was kept, and it was intended for the benefit of future generations if they would repent, as the title of the book implies. Enoch rejoiced that there was such a record, and that it had been handed down so that the people in his day might know how the word of the Lord

came to the fathers in the very beginning. Of this he wrote in his own record: "And death hath come upon our fathers; nevertheless we know them, and cannot deny, and even the first of all we know, even Adam. 

For a book of remembrance we have written among us, according to the pattern given by the finger of God; and it is given in our own language."

Abraham also had the record which came down from the fathers, and he rejoiced in the knowledge of the truth pertaining to the earth, the creation, and the Gospel. Moreover, he declared that it was his intention to add to this record the word of the Lord given in his day, that it might be of benefit to his posterity. 

A Noble, Uplifting Thought. How much more inspiring and noble is the thought that man, in the beginning of the world, was taught a perfect language in the presence of God, and that all who desired it were instructed to read and write in a language which was spoken in the heavens, than to believe, as many now do, in the foolish, stagnant philosophy of the present day, that man in the beginning could not even speak, much less read and write in a language that was pure and undefiled! Why men will follow the mistaken theories of the world, rather than accept the inspiration from the heavens, is strange for some of us to understand. We speak of the "dark ages" of the world, and the "revival of learning" which followed, and in this we speak truly, for the light which came in the beginning was taken

away and was followed by gross spiritual darkness, and then again, when the Lord saw fit to bring it about, he again revived the minds of men in preparation for the restoration of his divine truth in the Dispensation of the 

Fulness of Times. 

How Ancient Records Were Kept. In what man ner these ancient records were kept, history does not fully reveal. Whether the recorders wrote on parchment, on metal, or the tablets and cylinders of clay, the fact remains that records were kept and handed down from generation to generation from the days of Adam. We' know that Abraham kept his record on papyrus according to the custom of the Egyptians going back beyond his day. The Lord gave to Moses commandments, written by the finger of the Lord, on tables of stone, and we know that the Hebrews kept records on parchment and vellum, and also on metals, which was a custom among other nations. Henry George Bohn has said: "Writing seems also to have been common, at a very early period, on palm and olive leaves, and especially on the bark of trees- -a material used even in the present time in some parts of Asia. *

Tabula, which properly means a 'plank' or 'board,' now also signifies the plate of a book, and was so used by Addison, who calls his plates 'tables.' 

Folium ('a leaf') has given us the word 'folio;' and the word liber, originally meaning the inner bark of a tree,' was afterwards used by the Romans to signify a book; whence we derive our words, 'library,' 'librarian,' etc."

Much of our knowledge of the past coming from the nations which "knew not God," has been recorded on stones, baked clay tablets, and cylinders and broken pottery. There are some instances of records having been kept on plates of brass as well as of gold, confirming the account in the Book of Mormon. 

The Discovery of Printing. 

It is declared by many scholars that the discovery, or invention, of printing is the greatest of all inventions. Whether or not this be true, it must be conceded that printing has had a most powerful effect upon the civilization of the world. While the idea is erroneous that in ancient times very few of the people were educated enough to read and write, the incentive for knowledge increased with leaps and bounds after the invention of printing. It has been taught that few people could read and write before this time. It is true that in the "dark ages" this accomplishment was limited to comparatively few, but the reason for this is that such knowledge was denied the common people. 

They were instructed that such accomplishments were not for them, and that they should depend upon their priests for knowledge.

Movable Type of Modern Origin. Printing with movable type is of comparatively recent times. Printing from blocks and clay tablets was practiced in China and other lands before the Christian Era. The great discovery was that of forming each letter separately, so as to make possible the rearranging and forming of the words,

lines and pages of a printed book, thus avoiding the cutting of new blocks for each page. The claim of 

Johannes Gutenberg to the invention of this kind of type is generally conceded, although there has been some dispute in relation to this matter. Printing dates from about 1438. In the middle of the fifteenth century there were several books printed by means of movable type, and before 1500, printing presses had been set up in some 220 different places in Europe.

Knowledge of Paper Making. The art of printing would have been greatly hampered had it not been for the discovery of how to make paper. The making of paper dates from, at least, the twelfth century, and from very early times papyrus was used by the Egyptians, but the use of papyrus, and the costly parchments and tables of wood and stone could not be continued in the time of printing with any degree of success. It was about the time of the invention of printing that the perfecting of paper making out of rags was accomplished. This discovery did away with the expensive means of making books by hand which was the custom in the days when scribes had to write each volume with a pen. 

Early Publications. The first printed book from movable type was a Latin edition of the Bible which appeared between 1450 and 1455. We are informed that in the last thirty years of the fifteenth century 10,000 books and pamphlets were published in Europe. 

This does not appeal to us as being anything very re-

markable in this day when thousands of tons of printing are given to the public every day, but in that day it was a wonderful accomplishment.

The schoolmen, who were ecclestiastics, looked upon all learning as a part of theology and pertaining to the church. All matters were supposed to be settled by an appeal to the clergy, and they, in turn, were to interpret according to their understanding of the Scriptures. But since the Spirit of the Lord was not guiding these schoolmen they made, at times, serious mistakes in their interpretations. All of the writings, as we have said, were in the Latin tongue. In their day the Christian religion, says Frederick Seebohm, had been changed and that "which in the days of Christ and the apostles was a thing of the heart (love of God and man's neighbor), had grown into a theology—a thing of the head."

The desire of the clergy, which was impressed upon the people by physical force, was to prevent the common people from learning to read and write for fear they would demand the privilege of reading and interpreting for themselves the Bible. 

Henry George Bohn, discussing the "Origin of 

Printing," has this to say:

It may be wondered that Claxton (who was the earliest printer in England), like many of the foreign printers, did not begin with, or at least some time during his career, print the Scriptures, especially as Wycliffe's translation had already been made. But there were good reasons. Religious persecution ran high, and the clergy were extremely jealous of the propagation

of the Scriptures among the people. Knighton had denounced the reading the Bible, lamenting lest this jewel of the church, hitherto the exclusive property of the clergy and divines, should be made common to laity; and Archbishop Arundel had issued an enactment that no part of the Scriptures in English should be read, either in public or private, or be thereafter translated, under pain of the greater excommunication. The Star Chamber, too, was big with terrors. A little later, Erasmus' edition of the 

New Testament was forbidden at Cambridge, and in the country of Surrey the Vicar of Croydon said from the pulpit, "We must root out printing, or printing will root out us."

The Art of Printing Spreads Over the Earth. 

No matter how much pressure could be brought to bear by those in authority against printing and the reading of printed Bibles, the ability to read and write continued to grow among the people. The publication of books, including the Bible, was too great a force to be stemmed by the puny arm of tyranny. Like an irresistible flood, printing, and the desire to read what was printed, swept over the entire land. This flood-tide spelt the doom of the Latin language as the language of literature. Books in the everyday speech of the people began to multiply. 

Moreover, no matter how severe the threats were against the translating, printing and reading of the Bible in the native tongues, this work continued to go on, leaving in its wake, it is true, the mangled and charred bodies of those who had marked the way. 

Wycliffe gave to the English people their first translation of the Bible in their own tongue. 

This was before the days of printing. Others arose to carry on

his work. To Richard Grafton is due the credit of becoming the first publisher of the Bible in England. 

He did this at the peril of his life. His first publication was of Coverdale's and Tyndale's translations of the Bible. Printing of the Bible had to be done secretly in those days, and most of the time no one knew where it was done. As time went on and other changes came among the people, more and more copies of the Bible appeared in the vernacular tongues and the tide could not be stemmed. Finally, through the increase of light and freedom which was brought about by the "Reformers," the work of disseminating copies of the Bible among the people continued openly and steadily until all the people had the blessed privilege of owning copies of the Scriptures. So the time came that instead of it being considered a crime for a man to read the Bible to his family, which had to be done under cover and in fear of the most painful punishments, men were taught to reverently read the Scriptures. This reading was taught as a duty which every man owes to his faith and to God.

CHAPTER 18  

Bound by "the Chains of Hell." 

Alma, when instructing Zeezrom on things pertaining to the kingdom of God, said: "And they that will harden their hearts, to them is given the lesser portion of the word until they know nothing concerning his mysteries; and then they are taken captive by the devil, and led by his will down to destruction." Now this is what is meant by "the chains of hell." During the period spoken of as the "Dark Ages" Satan had bound the souls of men in the depths of ignorance and superstition. This condition had come about because men had hardened their hearts against the things of the kingdom of God. The prophetic words of Isaiah were verily fulfilled: "For, behold, the darkness shall cover the earth, and gross darkness the people."

There is no darkness so black and heavy as spiritual darkness. Such darkness comes through the transgression of divine law. There is no ignorance so fatal as the ignorance of the saving principles of the Gospel. 

Great learning does not always savor of salvation. The fact is that few among the learned are willing to accept the simple principles of divine truth. Well did the 

Savior say, "Except ye be converted, and become as

little children, ye shall not enter into the kindom of heaven." Jacob, the brother of Nephi, also said: "O the cunning plan of the evil one! O the vainness, and frailties, and the foolishness of men! When they are learned they think they are wise, and they hearken not unto the counsel of God, for they set it aside, supposing they know of themselves, wherefore, their wisdom is foolishness and it profiteth them not. And they shal perish. But to be learned is good if they hearken unto the counsels of God."

False Ecclesiastical Power. The awful condi tion of superstition and ignorance of the knowledge of God which covered the earth for so many centuries after the passing of the apostles, was encouraged and guided by those who professed to be representatives of the Son of God upon the earth. There had arisen in those times an ecclesiastical power which controlled kingdoms and principalities. It had restricted by severe and painful edicts the privileges and individual rights of religious worship. It had bound, as far as it had power to bind, the consciences of men. It endeavored to set the bounds of individual thinking within the narrow limits of its creed. It pried into the sacred and private relationships of the home. It decreed when and how a man should pray. It punished with excruciating agony many who defied its power. No king was safe upon his throne without its sanction and approval. By the Dictatus of 

Pope Gregory VII it was declared that the church,

"has never erred, nor will it err to all eternity," therefore all its edicts were to be obeyed, and it reserved the right to annul all other contracts, vows and obligations. 

Charles IV who, at the age of twenty, held dominion over the greater part of Europe, stood with his head and feet bare and scantily clothed, doing penance for three days in the midst of winter at Canossa, Italy, for giving offense to the ecclesiastical power. Such was the dominion exercised by this power over nations as well as the souls of men. 

During the 

Beginning of the "Reformation." fourteenth and fifteenth centuries many things happened to cause the people to question the absolute power and divine auhority of the Church of Rome. The spirit of independent thinking had been turned loose among the people. This spirit continued to increase until it gained flood-tide in the sixteenth century, and brought to pass a rupture in the existing Church which led nearly half of the people of Europe from its jurisdiction. One of the leading causes of this disruption was the existence of scandalous acts on the part of the church and its ministers. The selling of "indulgences" in a shameful manner with the promise of absolving men from sin, was another. Then again, kings and rulers were glad to break the yoke which bound them and this led some of them to give aid to the brave but rebellious men who defied this great power.

In the fourteenth century there came what is

called the Great Schism in the church. 

Several centuries previously there had been a split which had created the Eastern and Western Catholic Churches, but this did not stop the power and influence of the Pope of Rome in Western Europe. When the Great Schism came the church was again divided and two presiding heads were chosen, one with headquarters at Rome and one at Avignon in France. Later the attempt was made to depose both of these ecclesiastical rulers and make a third, and for a time there were three Popes, each claiming authority! This condition prevailed for some forty years. The squabbling and contentions among these church heads gave to the Protestant forces a power which they could not have had under other circumstances. It also had the effect of making many of the people dissatisfied, and so this schism became one of the important factors in bringing to pass the revolution in religion which took from this power much of its authority. "The Morning Star of the Reformation." John 

Wycliffe (or Wiclif) was born in England in 1320. 

He has been called "The Morning Star of the Reformation." He graduated from Oxford and then became professor in that university. He was a learned man and also a priest, and as a priest took part in public affairs. He became a notable preacher and writer and early in his life he began to doubt some of the doctrines of the Catholic Church. He was a brave man and openly

advocated his principles. This resulted in a summons for him to go to London to stand trial for heresy. He found a powerful friend in the Duke of Lancaster, who saved him at this time. The next year the Great Schism in the church began, and through this he was again protected, since the rival popes were too busy with their own affairs to bother him. This schism gave 

Wycliffe additional argument and power in his contention against the Popes. Through his zeal and knowledge he gathered many followers, who were known by their enemies as Lollards, or babblers. While later efforts were made to stamp out these Lollards, the effort failed before the days of the "Reformation," and then it was too late to deal with all the heretics. Wycliffe gave to the people a translation of the Bible in English which was widely read. He died in 1384, after having passed through a most eventful and stormy life, but a fortunate one because of the troubles existing in his time which saved him, without doubt, from being burned at the stake.

John Huss. After the death of Wycliffe his work was continued by John Huss, in Bohemia. These two men never met, but Huss obtained translations of Wycliffe's writings which had been spread all over Europe. Huss was also a teacher and a preacher. He declared that no man on earth had power to forgive sins, and he taught the people to search the Scriptures for the words of eternal life, a thing forbidden by the

Through his activity he ruling ecclesiastical power. converted thousands of his countrymen. He was summoned to a church council at Constance and unwisely went (although it was with the sacred promise of King 

Sigismund that he should be returned in safety), was condemned for heresy and in July, 1415, was burned at the stake. Because of his death the Bohemians rose in rebellion and carried on a war for several years which was finally concluded through a compromise. 

The spirit which Wycliffe and Huss had given to the movement of reform went on; it could not be stopped for there were unseen powers greater than the power of men at work among the people. The same council that condemned John Huss resorted to the expediency of taking the bones of Wycliffe from their resting place, burning them and then throwing the ashes in a stream called the Swift. It is written that "the Swift conveyed them into the Avon, the Avon into the Severn, the Severn into the narrow seas, and they into the main ocean; and thus the ashes of Wycliffe are the emblem of his doctrine, which is now scatered over all the world." 

Martin Luther. The foremost figure among the "reformers" was Martin Luther, an Augustine monk, and teacher in the University of Wittenberg, Germany.

Luther was born in Saxony in 1483, of humble parentage. Amidst the struggle which poverty imposed upon

him but with a determined will, he rose to be renowned as a scholar long before his rebellion against Rome. His study and reflections while in the service of the church awakened in his mind many grave doubts and he was led to question many of the doctrines of the church. He gradually reached the conclusion that the system of penance and forgiveness of sins through indulgences was wrong. Before this time, Erasmus had written his "Praise of Folly" in which he described indulgences as "the crime of false pardons," and he later wrote to his colleague Colet:

I have made up my mind to spend the remainder of my life with you in retirement from a world which is everywhere rotten. Ecclesiastical hypocrites rule in the courts of princes. 

The Court of Rome clearly has lost all sense of shame; for what could be more shameless than these continued indulgences! 

The Leaven At Work. We see that the leaven was at work which was destined to correct, in part at least, many of the abuses which prevailed at that time. 

Wittenberg was an old-fashioned town in Saxony. The 

University of Wittenberg at which Luther was an instructor had been founded by Frederick, the Elector of Saxony. In the year 1510, Luther visited Rome and in keeping with the custom at the time ascended the sacred stairs of the Lateran on his knees in penitence when he seemed to hear a voice saying: "The Just shall live by faith." While in. Rome he was shocked by the

scandalous stories being circulated about Pope Alexander VI and Julius II and the unchristian acts of many churchmen. These stories made an everlasting impression upon his mind. In October, 1517, a Dominican monk named Tetzel came to Saxony granting indulgences in that land as he had done in other lands. 

These indulgences were authorized by Pope Leo X for the purpose of raising funds for the construction of the great church of St. Peter in Rome. Those who purchased were expected to pay in proportion to their ability, thus the rich were required to pay a considerable sum for these benefits which were also extended to the poor for smaller sums. The result of months of serious thinking had brought Luther to the opinion that forgiveness of sins was due to humility and faith in God and could not be procured by a pardon obtained through the paying of sums of money. The activity and doctrine of 

Tetzel were challenged by Luther who wrote out ninetyfive theses, or statements, which he posted on the door of the Wittenberg Church inviting anyone so interested to engage in a discussion of the subject of indulgences with him. By posting these theses upon the door Luther had no intention of attacking the church but to correct what he saw an evil which had crept into the church. 

These ninety-five statements were written in Latin and therefore were addressed to scholars. They were soon copied and printed and circulated all over Europe, copies

coming to the hands of the ecclesiastical powers in Rome. 

The Pope's Bull. The result of this publication and the discussion of the matter by Luther soon brought to him a summons to appear at the papal court in Rome to answer for heresy. Knowing what had happened to 

Huss and Jerome of Prague, Luther was in no hurry to go to Rome. He found a powerful friend in Frederick,

Elector of Saxony, and the result was that Luther was permitted to consult with the papal emissaries in Germany.
